Intuitive Machines and Rocket Lab: A Comparison of Spiking Sales Versus Steady Revenue

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Intuitive Machines went flat for two years before tripling revenue, while Rocket Lab grew steadily -- but the gap is closing fast.

Intuitive Machines (LUNR -5.25%) primarily generates revenue by developing specialized aerospace products and delivering support services for robotic space exploration.

Between August and November, it secured tracking satellite contracts for the Space Development Agency and the Jet Propulsion Laboratory, and it completed the in-orbit commissioning of an SXM-11 spacecraft, while also booking a commercial order for two platform systems.

Rocket Lab (RKLB -3.79%) primarily earns revenue by providing orbital launch vehicle services and manufacturing advanced spacecraft components for global clients.

During the past three months, it completed its 99th mission for its Electron rocket, and finalized its largest commercial Electron launch contract to date, encompassing 20 launches. It also advanced its pending acquisition of Iridium Communications after stockholders approved the transaction and simultaneously adjusted the target launch timing for its developing Neutron vehicle.

Revenue shows investors the total money a business brings in before expenses. This metric reveals whether an organization is successfully attracting customers and growing its overall business volume over time.

Intuitive Machines vs. Rocket Lab Revenue over the last 8 quarters.

Data source: Financial Modeling Prep. Data as of Nov. 2, 2026.

The revenue trends for Intuitive Machines and Rocket Lab speak volumes about each's space-based businesses. Rocket Lab's steady sales growth over the past two years demonstrates its diversified revenue streams. While some of its income relies on government contracts, it has significant commercial launch sales.

Rocket Lab's success is evidenced by its consistent quarter-over-quarter top-line expansion since the start of 2025. This trend is expected to continue into the third quarter, with management forecasting revenue between $250 million and $265 million. The company's impending acquisition of Iridium Communications only adds to its aggressive revenue growth.

Intuitive Machines is heavily reliant on U.S. government contracts, leading to lumpy quarterly sales results. Its massive revenue jump in 2026 was driven by contract wins with agencies such as NASA, contributing to the company's record backlog of $1.8 billion at the end of the second quarter.

That said, Intuitive Machines' 2026 sales explosion was also helped by acquisitions, which should accelerate its commercial revenue growth. This will shift the company from a purely government-dependent lunar lander specialist into a diversified commercial space infrastructure provider.

Open-world creature-catching adventure RPG Honkai: Nexus Anima will launch for PC, iOS, and Android in spring 2027, miHoYo announced. A final closed beta test will begin on November 25. Sign-ups are available now via the official website.

Here is an overview of the test, via miHoYo:

In the Final Closed Beta, players take on the role of an Animaster and set out from the town of Iia on a brand-new adventure. This test features more captivating stories, distinctive gameplay, and richer exploration than any before it, painting a fuller picture of the world of Honkai: Nexus Anima. Players will not only unlock new chapters of the adventure, but also meet new faces along the way — some companions, others formidable rivals. Each brings a past and a stance of their own, adding more unknowns and possibilities to the journey ahead.

As the adventure unfolds, Iia, where the journey begins, will reveal more of itself: from the stately halls of a traditional bank, to the bustling floors of a high-rise office building, all the way to a breezy lighthouse island. Across these varied settings, players will get a feel for the distinct atmosphere and warmth of every corner of town. Out exploring, players may also come across Anima in their Chromatic forms. More juvenile Anima will join the adventure too, traveling at players’ side and facing challenges with them, so players can witness every step of their growth from fledgling to formidable. The Eidos Event Bond and Anima Event Bond are also being adjusted: the first 5-star Eidos or Anima a player obtains is guaranteed to be the current limited one, and obtaining the current limited Eidos also lets them claim the matching Anima Companion for free.

Beyond the joy of new encounters, this test also deepens the ways Anima keep players company—holding hands, hugging, being hoisted high in the air by an Anima. More of these interactions will unlock one by one, making every step together feel that much more real. Finally, combat is getting an upgrade too. The brand-new Nexus Arena mode officially launches: players can put together any lineup they like, get matched against opponents, and push for higher ranks. The open world and the various challenge stages will also see tougher enemies, for a more demanding combat experience.

The “Final Closed Beta” is a limited-slot test with a data wipe at the end, available on PC, Android, and iOS. Once players have filled out the recruitment questionnaire, a selection of them will be granted test access, and those selected will be notified through the eligibility checker, email, SMS, and other channels. Details of the test schedule will follow in later announcements.

Liverpool’s horror night underlines Andoni Iraola’s Champions League gamble

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Fenerbahce 3-2 Liverpool: Iraola defended making changes to his team but a late collapse in Istanbul highlighted the challenge of leading such a lopsided squad across multiple fronts

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

As Alexander Isak, Florian Wirtz and Cody Gakpo stood on the touchline in Istanbul, it was clear to see that Andoni Iraola needed some impact. Within minutes, he got it, as Isak headed in Gakpo’s cross to restore Liverpool’s lead at Fenerbahce. But Iraola’s triple-change did not resolve Liverpool’s biggest issue as they dropped points in the Champions League for the first time this season and slipped to consecutive defeats in all competitions: that his team were disorganised and lacked cohesion. As the visitors twice conceded late on to give up their lead for the second time, a 3-2 defeat to Dirk Kuyt’s side was Liverpool’s worst night since Iraola took charge.

It can put be down as a blip, particularly as the nature of the Champions League league phase – and Liverpool starting their campaign with three wins from three – meant they faced a hostile crowd and intimidating atmosphere in Istanbul but very little actual jeopardy. That was reflected in Iraola’s line-up as he tried out a strikerless attack that featured Bradley Barcola, Rio Ngumoha and Victor Munoz, with Isak and Gakpo kept in reserve. The move backfired. Barcola, so promising attacking from the left against Arsenal on Sunday, carried little threat as he played in the unfamiliar central role. Liverpool missed a presence up front, but it was also their lapses in defence that cost them.

“Cheap goals. Messy goals,” Iraola said. Liverpool conceded three times from set-pieces but if Mason Greenwood’s deflected free-kick was unlucky then losing Archie Brown at the back post from a corner was sloppy and Vedat Muriqi rising in the box following a long throw, surrounded by red shirts but otherwise unopposed, was just not good enough. It was also the third time in as many games in the Champions League that Liverpool had shipped three. They switched off late on while leading Lask in Austria before a dismal start against Villarreal at Anfield. At Fenerbahce, it felt as if there was an element of chaos throughout.

Afterwards, Iraola defended making five changes from Sunday’s 1-0 defeat to Arsenal. The nature of Liverpool’s lopsided squad has meant that Iraola has had to trust his options throughout a demanding period of six games in 18 days. At Fenerbahce, Iraola largely replicated the approach of Liverpool’s Carabao Cup win against Chelsea last week, keeping his first-choice attacking options back before gradually adding strength later on. But also making changes in defence – with Joe Gomez and Kostas Tsimikas withdrawn late on – was a gamble and did not help Liverpool’s security, instead adding to their instability.

“We have done this not just today, we have done it all season,” Iraola said in his defence. “All the games midweek we have been changing four or five as it’s impossible to retain the starting XI. I trust the squad to sustain the level and I don’t think it is the main reason why we lost. We have to look more at how we conceded the goals as the reason we didn’t win.”

Fenerbahce had lost 6-0 to Aston Villa just last month and were in a sorry state following the resignation of Ismail Kartal last week. No wonder interim boss Kuyt hailed victory over his former team as a “miracle”. But it was a wake-up call for Liverpool in the Champions League, too, while underlining the task Iraola faces to guide this curiously assembled squad on multiple fronts.

How Emirates Safeguards Its Longest Airbus A380 Route With A Little-Known Fallback

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Operating the world's longest Airbus A380 route is an achievement that demands far more than simply assigning the aircraft with the greatest passenger capacity. Every flight between Dubai International Airport (DXB) and Auckland Airport (AKL) requires careful planning, significant fuel reserves, favorable weather conditions, and enough operational flexibility to cope with changing winds across thousands of miles of ocean and remote airspace.

While the nonstop service often attracts attention because of its remarkable duration, the supporting strategy behind it is equally noteworthy. Since launching the route in 2016, Emirates has quietly maintained an alternative network that allows passengers to reach New Zealand even when the airline adjusts nonstop operations.

Rather than relying exclusively on a single ultra-long-haul flight, the Dubai-based carrier has preserved one-stop connections through Australia, creating a practical fallback that strengthens resilience while giving planners greater freedom to respond to operational challenges and changing market demand.

The nonstop route between DXB and AKL has occupied a unique position within Emirates' network since its introduction in March 2016. Covering approximately 8,800 miles (14,162 km), the flight represents the airline's longest scheduled A380 operation and remains among the longest commercial flights operated anywhere in the world.

Although the eastbound journey from Auckland to Dubai is lengthy in its own right, the westbound flight usually presents the greater challenge because of prevailing winds across the Indian Ocean and Southeast Asia. Under favorable conditions, the journey can be completed in a little over 17 hours, but strong headwinds have regularly extended block times to around 17 hours and 25 minutes.

This makes every departure a carefully planned balancing act involving payload, fuel, weather forecasts, and air traffic considerations. These operational realities mean that dispatchers cannot simply schedule the same aircraft performance every day throughout the year. Seasonal wind patterns influence fuel requirements, while unexpected weather systems may require revised routings that add additional flying time.

Even relatively small changes become significant on flights lasting well over 17 hours, where every additional minute increases fuel consumption and operational complexity. Despite these challenges, Emirates has continued to assign the A380 exclusively to the route.

This highlights the carrier's confidence in the aircraft's capabilities. The double-decker provides substantial passenger capacity across premium and economy cabins while also offering the range needed for one of commercial aviation's most demanding missions.

While the nonstop route receives the majority of public attention, Emirates has never depended on it as the sole means of serving Auckland. Instead, the airline has continued operating extensive services into Australia's largest cities, allowing passengers to connect onward to New Zealand whenever necessary.

Sydney Kingsford Smith Airport (SYD) has traditionally played the most prominent role within this strategy thanks to its extensive schedule from Dubai and its large volume of connecting passengers. Emirates also maintains significant operations into Melbourne Airport (MEL) and Brisbane Airport (BNE), creating multiple pathways for travelers heading toward Auckland without requiring the airline to depend entirely on the nonstop service.

This approach gives Emirates an established backup within its broader network rather than requiring special contingency planning whenever operational conditions become less favorable. If schedules change or nonstop frequencies are reduced, passengers can still travel between Dubai and Auckland through Australian gateways using connections that have long been integrated into the airline's system.

The arrangement also benefits customers originating outside Dubai, as Emirates carries passengers from across Europe, the Middle East, Africa, and Asia through its Dubai hub before distributing them across its long-haul network. Having several onward routing options increases flexibility throughout the reservation system and helps minimize disruption when demand patterns shift.

Flights lasting more than 17 hours leave little room for operational uncertainty. Airline planners must consider aircraft performance, passenger loads, cargo demand, weather forecasts, alternate airports, and regulatory fuel requirements before every departure. Unlike shorter international routes, adjustments cannot easily be absorbed once the aircraft is airborne because diversion options remain limited across large stretches of ocean.

Headwinds represent one of the most important variables affecting Dubai-Auckland operations, and strong winds opposing the aircraft increase fuel burn and can reduce operational margins, particularly on the westbound sector. As a result, planners continuously evaluate expected atmospheric conditions before finalizing payload and fuel calculations.

Maintaining one-stop alternatives through Australia provides an additional layer of flexibility within this process, as rather than insisting that every passenger travel nonstop regardless of operating conditions, Emirates can adapt schedules according to seasonal requirements while continuing to offer reliable service between Dubai and New Zealand.

The strategy also supports efficient fleet utilization. A380 examples represent valuable assets that must be allocated carefully across the airline's global network. Having multiple routing options allows Emirates to deploy aircraft where demand is strongest while preserving connectivity even when operational priorities evolve during different times of the year.

Four out of the seven routes use the Boeing 787.

Although the Australian connection strategy remains in place, passenger demand has increasingly favored the nonstop option. As international travel continued to recover and long-haul markets strengthened, Emirates gradually expanded nonstop operations between Dubai and Auckland.

A significant milestone arrived earlier this year, when the airline increased the route to daily A380 service. The schedule expansion reflected confidence in sustained demand while demonstrating that the economics of operating one of the world's longest flights remained attractive despite its considerable operating costs.

Even with daily nonstop flights, however, the underlying network philosophy has not changed. The Australian gateways continue serving an important role by providing additional capacity, supporting passengers whose itineraries involve Australian destinations, and preserving operational resilience whenever circumstances require adjustments. Rather than replacing the connecting model, the expanded non-stop schedule complements it.

Together, both options create a network that can accommodate different passenger preferences while allowing Emirates to respond to changes in demand without sacrificing connectivity across the region. The result is a system that balances efficiency with redundancy, ensuring that one of the airline's most ambitious routes remains supported by a broader operational framework rather than existing in isolation.

Many airlines have reduced or retired A380 fleets over the past decade, yet Emirates has remained firmly committed to the type as the flagship of its long-haul operation. No carrier operates more A380s, and the aircraft continues serving numerous high-demand destinations throughout Europe, Asia, Australia, and North America.

The Dubai-Auckland route showcases many of the aircraft's strengths. Its combination of long range and high seating capacity allows Emirates to transport hundreds of passengers on a single flight while offering premium products that include onboard lounges and shower spas for first-class travelers.

Operating such a large aircraft on an ultra-long-haul route also improves efficiency when passenger demand supports high load factors. Instead of scheduling multiple smaller aircraft, Emirates can concentrate capacity into a single daily departure while maintaining a consistent onboard product across the route. The airline's continuing investment in cabin refurbishment programs further reinforces the importance of the A380 within its future plans.

Updated interiors, refreshed premium cabins, and modernized economy seating help ensure that the aircraft remains competitive despite its age, particularly on flights where passenger comfort becomes increasingly important after more than 17 hours in the air. As detailed in the table above, Emirates currently has a total of 116 Airbus A380s in its fleet, according to ch-aviation.

Why the Airbus A350-1000’s range, efficiency, and comfort can make it the ideal aircraft for Emirates’ ultra-long-haul global network.

From a passenger perspective, the non-stop Dubai-Auckland service appears to be a single record-setting flight linking two distant cities. Behind the scenes, however, it functions as one component within a much larger network built around flexibility, redundancy, and efficient aircraft utilization.

The continued availability of Australian connections demonstrates that successful ultra-long-haul operations depend as much on network planning as aircraft performance. By maintaining multiple routing options through Sydney, Melbourne, and Brisbane, Emirates reduces dependence on any single schedule while preserving service continuity across the broader region.

This philosophy has allowed the airline to adjust non-stop frequencies over time without abandoning the market or compromising connectivity. Whether operating fewer non-stop flights during certain periods or increasing the service to daily A380 departures when demand supports expansion, Emirates retains an established fallback that has been part of its strategy since the route first launched in 2016.

Rather than viewing the Australian services as competitors to the non-stop flight, Emirates has positioned them as complementary components of a broader network. That combination enables the airline to continue operating one of commercial aviation's most demanding Airbus A380 routes while maintaining the operational resilience needed for flights that regularly spend well over 17 hours in the air.

Ridley Scott's Apple TV Crime Thriller Is a 10/10 Binge-Watch

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

A crime series needs certain qualities to work. Of course, there have to be twisty plots, plenty of exciting and suspenseful moments, and characters that you become instantly drawn to. Recent shows like True Detective, Task, and MobLand have all introduced viewers to thrilling episodes that provide an inside look at dark, mysterious criminal underworlds. One series in particular flew under the radar when it was released, but remains every bit as worthy of a binge-watch as the other shows in this genre.

The Ridley Scott-produced drama Dope Thief premiered on Apple TV in March 2025. Over a succinct eight episodes, the Peter Craig-created series never shies away from creating an intense and brutally violent yet completely captivating story. The series was adapted from a 2009 novel by Dennis Tafoya, and follows two best friends named Manny Carvalho (Wagner Moura) and Ray Driscoll (Brian Tyree Henry), who are trying to survive on the rough streets of Philadelphia. They've created a small-time robbery scheme where they pose as DEA agents and steal from local drug dealers.

After you watch 'Dope Thief,' go back and watch these shows!

Their con works pretty well until they take on a job that brings their whole lives crashing down. Even though there are plenty of red flags from the start, Ray and Manny rob a drug operation that is actually already being surveilled by real DEA officers. From there, the two men fall deeper and deeper into a massive criminal enterprise that puts them in danger at every turn. Can Manny and Ray outrun their fates, and will their friendship survive the danger that's coming for them? Each episode is fast-paced and drama-packed, but Dope Thief also manages to infuse its story with a wry humor that makes all the bloodshed a tiny bit easier to stomach.

Dope Thief stands apart from other crime shows because of its impressive blend of genres. One moment, you'll be covering your eyes at the violence, while a moment later, you'll be chuckling at the silly antics Manny and Ray are forced to perform to escape danger. Despite the humor and levity, Dope Thief never shies away from emphasizing the story's high-stakes circumstances. The series also impressively emphasizes the importance of found family. The bonds between many characters are compelling and authentic, and although most aren't related by blood, their connections are just as powerful. Dope Thief also successfully portrays the long-lasting effects of trauma and the heartbreak of knowing someone struggling with addiction, weaving these elements to make each episode better than the last.

Much of Dope Thief's success is tied to Wagner and Henry's performances. Their chemistry is both electric and effortless, and each actor proves why they're considered among the best in the industry right now. It's no surprise that Henry was nominated for an Emmy for his work as Ray. Marin Ireland is also incredible as Mina, a beautifully broken undercover agent who becomes entangled in Ray and Manny's lives. We'd be remiss if we didn't mention the stellar performances of Kate Mulgrew as Ray's tough-as-nails adoptive mother and Ving Rhames as Ray's gruff, embattled father.

For more than a year, fans wondered if there would be future episodes of Dope Thief. However, a few weeks ago, it was announced that Apple TV wouldn't be moving forward with the series. Although it's a shame that there'll be no Season 2, you should still check out this exceptional show. With an impressive 86% Rotten Tomatoes score and edge-of-your-seat storytelling, Dope Thief is the perfect addition to the crime thriller genre.

Denzel Washington's Sci-Fi Crime Thriller 'Déjà Vu' Is 'Minority Report' Meets 'Looper'

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

If you’re in the mood for a fast-paced action thriller that goes in a surprising speculative direction, then you need to jump over to Tubi for one of Denzel Washington's greatest on-screen exploits. Currently free to stream on the platform is Déjà Vu, a post-9/11, post-Hurricane Katrina thriller that speaks to nationwide fears at the time, infusing them with a sci-fi twist that elevates the film to be more than just your average action flick. If it's been a few years, now is the perfect time to revisit the picture, as November marks the 20th anniversary of this Tony Scott-directed classic.

Like most of his features, Déjà Vu boasts Scott's signature style of kinetic energy and hyper-focused characters, as ATF agent Doug Carlin (Washington) struggles to catch a domestic terrorist, Carroll Oerstadt (Jim Caviezel), a self-appointed "patriot" who bombed a New Orleans ferry containing U.S. Navy sailors and their families. Soon after the attack, FBI agent Paul Pryzwarra (Val Kilmer) brings Carlin aboard his specialized task force centered around a secret government project that may help catch the killer. As it turns out, scientists have accidentally created a window in time—a window that offers Carlin the chance to stop the tragedy before it occurs. The only problem is that they can only look back just over four days, and those days pass by in real time with no ability to rewind. With little else to do but study the life of victim Claire Kuchever (Paula Patton), Carlin comes to recognize both the killer and his role in stopping him.

Déjà Vu is one of those action-thrillers that has you on the edge of your seat the entire time. Although the worst of it has already happened, the film treads in reverse as it attempts to put all the pieces together, intelligently weaving a narrative where the past and present intersect. This, naturally, leads to some interesting philosophical, as well as theological, conundrums, as Carlin and the team debate the ethics of such government surveillance (after the conspiratorial Enemy of the State, but before The Dark Knight did the same) and the moral uncertainty that arises from operating a window into the past: If God allows such a window to exist, is it not so that Carlin and company can then use it to not just solve a murder, but perhaps prevent one? These are the questions that anchor Déjà Vu, presenting the audience with some complex choices as time rapidly slips through the hourglass and the window into the recent past moves closer to the present tragedy.

Second watches are for catching everything you were too stressed to notice the first time.

Of all five collaborations between Washington and Scott, Déjà Vu is perhaps the most creatively engaging. (Yes, that's even considering the truly remarkable Man on Fire.) The 2006 thriller uses Scott's distinct visual style to go beyond the simple action thriller, bending genre and making way-out speculative concepts palatable for standard action audiences. At the same time, Washington toes the line between romance and duty as he begins to fall for the woman who, in his timeline, is already dead. It's a brilliant concept with impeccable execution that goes beyond the pair's usual brand of grounded, fast-paced thrillers.

As such, Déjà Vu offers the viewer plenty of thrills, chills, and philosophical pondering, and pits them against a ticking clock that screams "already, but not yet." It's an exceptional film that perfectly encapsulates the anxieties of early 2000s America while not grounding itself in them so deeply that it feels outdated or outpaced. Indeed, it's the type of film that they don't seem to make anymore—a mid-budget thriller with a stacked cast and great thematic potential—and that's a real shame. Tony Scott was a visionary filmmaker who never let genre (or anything, really) constrain his vision, and with Denzel Washington in the leading role, Déjà Vu is still just as great today as it was 20 years ago.

Déjà Vu is available for streaming on Tubi.

5 Sci-Fi Books You Can Finish in a Weekend

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Sci-fi can be a lot to take in, and that's understandable. Luckily, some of the best sci-fi books can be finished in two days or less. From time travel and alternate universes to human-made dystopias, the genre explores familiar and unfamiliar concepts, imagining worlds where technology seems to have no bounds and human intelligence has reached unimaginable heights.

But progress comes with consequences, no matter how logical a decision might seem. Science cannot exist without the humans who practice it, and ultimately, their morals determine whether it is used for good or bad. That's some pretty heavy philosophical stuff, but certain writers know how to tackle these ideas in shorter books or through plots that are a breeze to read. From an absurdly ridiculous take on alien encounters to a survival thriller set on Mars, here are sci-fi books you can finish in a weekend.

Winner of the 2020 Hugo Award for Best Novella, Amal El-Mohtar and Max Gladstone's This Is How You Lose the Time War follows Blue and Red, two rival agents working for opposing organizations across different timelines and universes in a post-apocalyptic future. Both women are tasked with manipulating events in the past to secure the future their respective factions desire. Although they've crossed paths countless times during their missions, they've never exchanged a word — that is, until Blue breaks the routine (and the rules) by sending Red a letter. What begins as a simple exchange soon blossoms into a companionship that puts their reputations, loyalties, and duties at risk.

At approximately 200 pages, This Is How You Lose the Time War is told in an epistolary format, which makes sense given that Blue and Red's main form of communication is through letters. This also makes the novel an easily digestible read that you can finish in two days. However, its shorter length doesn't take away from the ambitious scope of the story, especially when concepts like branching timelines and alternate universes are often reserved for longer novels with extensive worldbuilding. But this novel is more than just sci-fi. It's also about a forbidden love with consequences that extend beyond the two people involved. Their relationship could potentially threaten the fragile nature of the universes themselves, showing how something as personal as love can have far-reaching scientific implications that put humanity at risk.

At around 200 pages, Douglas Adams' The Hitchhiker’s Guide to the Galaxy stuffs an entire universe of ridiculous adventures into a surprisingly quick read. It all starts when Earth is demolished by the Vogons to make room for a hyperspace bypass, leaving ordinary Englishman Arthur Dent scrambling to survive. He escapes with Ford Prefect, an alien friend who takes him across the galaxy alongside a bizarre group of companions: the two-headed president Zaphod Beeblebrox, the chronically depressed robot Marvin, and Trillian, another human survivor. Their journey eventually brings Arthur closer to discovering Earth's actual purpose, which, naturally, involves a supercomputer.

Adams wastes little time making it clear that this isn't your typical sci-fi adventure. The universe operates on its own ridiculous logic, whether that means destroying an entire planet for a space shortcut or electing a two-headed president. The jokes come so frequently that readers barely have time to question the absurdity before the next one arrives. Beneath all that humor, the novel also takes a swipe at humanity's obsession with finding meaning in everything. People spend their lives searching for the ultimate answer to existence, only for the universe to offer very little in return. And when the supercomputer finally delivers its answer, the result is so hilariously underwhelming that it makes the whole search even funnier.

At just 84 to 176 pages, depending on the edition, H. G. Wells' The Time Machine is a Victorian-era predecessor to Back to the Future. The novella follows an eccentric English scientist known only as the Time Traveller, who builds a machine capable of traveling through time. Curious about humanity's future, he journeys to the year 802,701, where he encounters the Eloi, a seemingly carefree society living among the remnants of an advanced civilization. However, the world he discovers is far more complicated than it first appears, forcing him to reconsider what humanity’s future might look like.

Released during the Second Industrial Revolution, The Time Machine explores how technology shapes humanity and its relationship with nature. While these forces continue to intersect in the 21st century, seeing them through a late 19th-century perspective offers a fascinating glimpse into Victorian anxieties about progress. During this period, technological advancement was often seen as the key to Britain's continued dominance, reflecting the belief that humanity would only grow smarter, stronger, and more powerful. However, the Time Traveller's journey challenges that assumption. The book shows that human evolution has no guaranteed endpoint, and reaching what seems like the pinnacle of progress doesn't mean humanity's future will be any brighter.

Four ships, four completely different reasons to be up there at all. Here is the one that has your seat, what flying with them would cost you, and what it would give back.

You belong on the fastest piece of junk in the galaxy, with a crew that took the job for the payday and then quietly stopped caring about the payday. Nothing on the Falcon has ever gone according to plan, and everybody aboard prefers it that way.

You belong with the crew that turned a tiny cell of misfits into an actual rebellion. The Ghost is a home before it is a ship, and everybody who boards it arrived with something broken that the rest of them decided to look after.

You belong with the four kids who found a buried ship, launched it by accident and are now extremely lost in a galaxy that eats people like them. Nobody here knows what they are doing, and that turns out to be the fun of it.

You belong in the quietest ship in the galaxy, with one small passenger and a code you have never explained to anybody. You told yourself this was a job, and then it stopped being a job somewhere around the second week.

At roughly 370 pages, Andy Weir's The Martian is longer than some entries on this list, but its brisk pace—and the light, conversational tone from the lead character himself—makes it easy to keep turning pages. Mark Watney is on NASA's Ares 3 mission when a dust storm forces the crew to abandon Mars. An accident leaves him injured and separated from the others, who assume he is dead and head home. When Mark wakes up, he finds himself alone, with limited supplies, a damaged spacesuit, and no way to contact Earth. His only chance is to use his scientific knowledge to solve problems and find a way back.

The setup may sound like a straightforward survival thriller, but Weir gives the story an emotional purpose. Mark's resourcefulness keeps him alive, while the rescue effort shows how far people will go for someone they refuse to abandon. Back on Earth, NASA must decide whether another mission and its crew should be put at risk to bring him home. That dilemma gives the novel its heart. For all its science and engineering, The Martian comes down to one moral lesson: one person's life matters, even when saving it demands sacrifice. It's a reminder that survival is less of a solo achievement and more about people refusing to give up on you.

Reading is a crime in Ray Bradbury's Fahrenheit 451—arguably treated as more serious than murder. Anyone caught hiding, distributing, or even reading a book risks imprisonment. Firemen have an unusual job in this society: instead of putting out fires, they set books alight. Guy Montag has never questioned this arrangement until he meets Clarisse, a young neighbor whose curiosity about life challenges everything he has accepted as normal. His doubts deepen after he witnesses a woman choose to die with her books rather than surrender them. The encounter forces him to reconsider his job and the society he has served.

Depending on the edition, Fahrenheit 451 runs between 156 and 256 pages, but Bradbury packs a chillingly prophetic vision into that length. Published in the 1950s, the novel imagines a society that feels uncomfortably familiar today, from attempts to ban books deemed "controversial" to people spending hours glued to their phone screens. It also raises concerns about political forces seeking to silence criticism and discourage independent thought. Bradbury's warning is not simply that technology can distract people from the world. In the wrong hands, it can also become a tool for enforcing conformity, keeping the public occupied while those in power avoid scrutiny. Anyone who challenges that system risks consequences.

Sector Snapshot: Construction Robotics Funding Soars To $1.6B, Tripling Previous Peak

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Construction companies are struggling to find enough workers. For startups, that means venture investors are pouring money into companies betting that robots can help close the gap.

Companies spanning construction and robotics have raised about $1.6 billion this year through Oct. 29 — more than 4x the $389 million recorded in all of 2025 — according to Crunchbase data. The surge was driven by three companies that raised the majority of the funding.

The broad trend: For contractors struggling to hire, the appeal of construction-oriented tech offerings is straightforward: They need to get work done and there aren’t enough skilled workers to do it. The AI-driven data center building boom is only adding to that competition for workers. Labor shortages remain the biggest cause of project delays, according to a recent survey by the Associated General Contractors of America and NCCER.

Some of the technology is already at work on job sites.

San Francisco-based Bedrock Robotics, this year’s largest funding recipient in the sector, said in August that excavators equipped with its autonomous systems were operating without human operators on commercial projects in Texas and Nevada.

The numbers: With roughly two months left in the year, funding has already surpassed the previous five years combined.

Companies spanning construction and robotics raised about $1.6 billion through Oct. 29, 3x the previous high of $506 million in 2021, according to our dataset that goes back to 2018. This year’s total is also more than 3x the 2021 high of $506 million.

So far, the funding surge reflects much bigger bets rather than more deals. The sector has recorded 30 rounds this year, compared with 28 in all of 2025. Bedrock alone accounts for $870 million across two deals — more than half of this year’s total. This underscores just how much a few large financings are impacting the recovery.

Austin-based Icon, which builds homes using 3D printing, also raised two rounds in 2026. It has raised nearly $800 million since its 2017 inception. A company spokesperson told Crunchbase News that the company has taken its technology from an experiment to “an in-the-field system that can build faster, with fewer people, less waste, greater design freedom and lower cost.”

To date, the company has built more than 270 homes and structures across five states and two countries. It says that it’s on pace to grow revenue by 4x this year.

“Early on, the question was whether a robotic construction system could build a good home,” the spokesperson wrote via email. “Today, the question is whether a company can deliver at volume, across geographies and project types, and improve its economics with each build. That’s only answerable with time in the field.”

Bedrock Robotics was the largest funding recipient in 2026, impressively raising $870 million across two rounds. The 2-year-old company, which converts existing heavy construction equipment into autonomous machines, announced a $600 million Series C in October led by CapitalG and Emergence Capital. That round, which valued Bedrock at $4.1 billion, followed a $270 million Series B in February led by CapitalG and Valor Atreides AI Fund.

Austin-based construction technology company Icon also raised two rounds, bringing its listed 2026 funding to $265 million. Its largest financing was a $200 million Series D in October led by Baillie Gifford and BlackRock, following a $65 million Series C in March.

Another $200 million round went to Gravis Robotics, based in Zurich, Switzerland, which develops technology to retrofit construction machinery for autonomous earthmoving and excavation. SoftBank led the company’s Series A in August, which valued the startup at $1 billion post-money.

Beyond those large financings, investors backed companies automating homebuilding and specific construction tasks.

London-based All3, which uses AI and robotics to design, manufacture and assemble buildings, raised $25 million in April led by RTP Global. Origin, based in San Francisco, developing a general-purpose construction robot, secured $20 million in October, led by Accel and Zacua Ventures.

Acquisitions have dominated exit activity in construction robotics and related jobsite automation this year.

One of the largest disclosed deals was Carpinteria, California-based Procore’s acquisition of DroneDeploy, a San Francisco-based platform that combines imagery from drones, ground robots and cameras to track construction progress and site conditions. Procore announced the approximately $845 million cash transaction in July and closed it in September.

Other buyers have focused on automating the work itself. In January, Oshkosh subsidiary JLG Industries acquired the core technology developed by Canvas, a San Francisco-based startup known for robotic drywall finishing.

Heavy-equipment autonomy also attracted buyers. In March, HavocAI completed its acquisition of Palo Alto, California-based Teleo, whose technology lets operators remotely oversee heavy machinery.

The following month, San Francisco-based Pronto confirmed that Atoms, Travis Kalanick’s physical AI company, had completed its acquisition for an undisclosed price. Pronto’s autonomous haulage technology now reportedly underpins Atoms’ mining division.

Public offerings have been less prominent, with no known IPOs this year by companies focused specifically on construction robotics. The broader construction technology market did produce a sizable debut: Columbia, Missouri-based EquipmentShare, an equipment rental and connected-jobsite technology company, raised approximately $747 million in gross proceeds in its January IPO, selling 30.5 million shares at $24.50 each.

For all the variety in the companies attracting funding, this year’s surge has so far largely rested on three: Bedrock, Icon and Gravis, which together accounted for more than $1.3 billion, or about 83%, of the roughly $1.6 billion raised. Investors are placing unusually large bets on a small group of companies, even as acquisitions give others a route into larger businesses already serving construction customers.

Stay up to date with recent funding rounds, acquisitions, and more with the Crunchbase Daily.

Barcelona’s Kabilio raises €10M to bring AI agents to accounting firms

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Picus Capital and Visionaries led the round as the startup nears 500 client firms, up from 100 a year ago.

Kabilio co-founders and co-CEOs Jose Ojeda and Álex Valls

Barcelona startup Kabilio, which wants AI to take the receipt-chasing out of accounting, has raised €10M in a round led by Picus Capital and Visionaries. CDTI, Spain’s public agency for technology and innovation, also took part.

Both lead investors also backed the company’s €4m pre-seed round a year ago, and the new money brings its total funding to €14m. Since then, Kabilio has grown from about 100 accounting firms to nearly 500, and its team to 80 people.

Those firms do a job most small businesses would rather not. Across Spain and much of Europe, small companies pass their books and taxes to an outside firm. There, much of the work still means sorting invoices and matching payments by hand.

Kabilio started there. Its software reads invoices, suggests how to book them, matches bank transactions and flags anything odd, while the accountant reviews and signs off. Kabilio says this saves accountants about 60% of their bookkeeping and reconciliation time.

“Nobody becomes an accountant to chase receipts, and nobody starts a business to do paperwork,” said Jose Ojeda, Kabilio’s co-founder and co-CEO.

The new capital pays for the next step: an AI agent that firms can hand whole tasks to, much as they would a new hire. It will gather invoices and bank statements from inboxes, reconcile accounts, check entries and chase gaps, even messaging clients by email or WhatsApp.

The agent enters beta this month, and several firms are already testing it. Accountants keep the reins, giving it instructions in plain language and deciding, client by client, how much it may do on its own.

Kabilio is also bringing the firms’ clients onto the same platform. Kabilio Empresas, launched in September, lets small businesses invoice, link their bank accounts and watch their cash flow in the system their accountant uses. The company then sees new figures as they arrive instead of at tax time.

Later, the company plans to turn the agent towards those businesses too, sending invoices, nudging late payers and answering everyday tax questions for owners. Clients join only through their accountant, who sets the agent’s limits.

“An agent is only as good as the data it works on,” said Álex Valls, Kabilio’s other co-founder and co-CEO.

Kabilio is entering a busy field, as belgian startup Ravical raised a $17m seed round last week for its own AI platform for accounting firms, while Visa is piloting AI agents that pay supplier invoices.

Kitchen of the Week: A New Build that Eschews Perfection and Allows a ‘Boisterous’ Life

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

From house tours and expert advice to favorite finds and DIYs.

Design-forward shops, hotels, and restaurants for your itinerary.

Renovating? Start here for expert advice, deep dives, and helpful tips.

Our catalog of more than 10,000 products featured on Remodelista.

Remodelista’s members-only directory of architects, designers, and landscape professionals.

When we spotted Landed Interiors‘ recent project in Boulder, Colorado, we assumed the Berkeley-based design studio had been brought on to renovate an old farmhouse. With its thoughtful touches, appealing quirks, and cozy rooms, the home feels very much rooted in place and heritage, as if it has been knocking around for generations. Turns out, the entire thing was a new build.

How did Heather Menegat, the lead on the project, pull off the magic act? By working closely with the architects and builders at Coburn Partners, who were as interested in getting the details right as Heather was. And by choosing materials that are “characterful, with lots of pits and chips and dings,” she says. “They are perfect for this home where people and animals are constantly running in and out of the house.”

“In the kitchen, we sourced reclaimed wood for the paneling, and the flooring is a lovely, softly polished terracotta tile that always looks a little dirty even if you’ve mopped it recently,” she continues. “To balance these, we stuck to quieter surfaces for the moldings, cabinetry, ceiling, and countertops. Nothing is pristine (even the exposed drywall was skim-coated in plaster to make its surface more wonky), but the contrast keeps the room from feeling too busy and creates a subtle intrigue. There is an implication of layering and evolution—that someone intervened after the home was ‘built.’ ”

“I think that sometimes people assume that a higher price tag implies surfaces that are aiming for a particular kind of shelter magazine perfection or glamour. That can certainly be true, but for this project we selected high-quality, durable, and forgiving materials that would patina gracefully and allow the family to live boisterously without worry. It suits their needs.”

Below, Heather shares all the elements that went into creating this exceedingly sweet, much-used hearth of the home.

Is Gardening Art? Fergus Garrett Makes a Compelling Case

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

All Gardenista stories—from garden tours and expert advice to hand tools and furniture roundups.

Starting from scratch or upgrading an outdoor space? Look here for advice on plants and hardscape materials. We have 200 guides on everything from fences to foxgloves.

Heading somewhere? Plan your trip with our destination guides to our favorite public gardens, hotels, restaurants, and shops.

Gardenista’s members-only directory of landscape architects and garden designers.

See our obsessively curated catalog of favorite products sourced by the Gardenista editors.

Over the past few years some designers have preferred to label themselves as artists, perhaps viewing their outputs on a different plane from those by their fellow creatives. It’s a long-running debate on what is deemed worthy of being described “art”; in the late 19th century, it was a hot topic, as the Arts & Crafts movement believed that beautiful design and decorative arts were as worthy of discussion and reverence as fine art or sculpture. What about gardening? Could the creation of a garden be considered an art form, too?

In the hands of Fergus Garrett—who celebrated 30 years as head gardener at Great Dixter in Sussex, England, three years ago—there’s a strong case to suggest it could be. Evidence that Garrett is operating on a higher plane was in full effect on a recent midsummer visit to the Arts & Crafts house. As borders exploded in an intoxicating race to their high-summer peak, and beautifully orchestrated plants spilled out over paths, it was, as often happens in this garden, almost too much to take in. A visit to Great Dixter can feel like a slightly transcendental experience, via sensory overload and sheer beauty.

N.B.: This post was first published June 2023.

Crédit Mutuel Arkéa confirms cyberattack after ZachXBT flags $133M in suspected losses

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Crédit Mutuel Arkéa did not confirm the exact amount of losses but validated the order of magnitude of the $133 million figure flagged by ZachXBT.

French bank Crédit Mutuel Arkéa has confirmed that a cyberattack enabled fraudulent transfers from its account, which were converted into crypto via specialized platforms.

In a statement released Wednesday, the bank said that client assets were unaffected and the incident has been contained.

"As soon as this attack was identified, our teams immediately activated the necessary security measures to contain the incident and prevent any further fraudulent movements against Crédit Mutuel Arkéa," the bank said.

"We wish to reassure all of our customers: this incident has had no impact on their assets," the bank added. "It only caused a delay in the execution of certain transfers."

Onchain sleuth ZachXBT, who first publicly flagged the exploit on Nov. 2, estimated the losses at more than $133 million. He noted that the figure could rise, as the attack unfolded over several days and may have involved additional over-the-counter transactions.

While Crédit Mutuel Arkéa did not confirm the exact amount of losses, it validated the order of magnitude. The bank said some of the stolen funds had been blocked.

"While it is therefore still too early to communicate a final amount, we can already state that the amounts involved are in no way of a nature to undermine the financial strength and soundness of Crédit Mutuel Arkéa," the bank said.

ZachXBT previously reported that the attacker had been moving stolen funds through a series of transactions, including swapping USDC for ETH, routing assets through instant exchanges such as WellWallet and Kyrrex, converting funds into XMR via Wagyu, and bridging assets across multiple chains.

He also said he has a "high degree of confidence" that the attacker was not associated with North Korea's Lazarus Group.

The bank has over 12,000 employees and more than 5.8 million members and customers. Its reported total assets are €211.5 billion ($238.1 billion), according to the statement.

An editor checks the facts, figures and quotes they produce against primary sources, and every piece is reviewed by an editor before it is published. Sponsored content and press releases are labeled as such and are not written by our editorial team.

The New Congress Can Repair Civil-Military Relations

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

PETER D. FEAVER is Professor of Political Science and Public Policy at Duke University and the author of Thanks for Your Service: The Causes and Consequences of Public Confidence in the U.S. Military.

HEIDI URBEN is Professor of the Practice and Associate Director of Georgetown University’s Center for Security Studies in the Walsh School of Foreign Service.

How It Can Reclaim Its Constitutional Powers

Article I of the Constitution gives Congress sweeping powers to raise, maintain, and equip the armed forces. But the current Congress has done little to use these powers. The intense, frenetic activity of the past two years has undone established norms for military professionalism and may shape civil-military relations for a generation. Yet it has been led almost entirely by the executive branch with little meaningful involvement or pushback from Capitol Hill.

The next Congress will face great pressure to reverse course and in doing so could shore up the historical norms of American civil-military relations. The concern today is not a military that is defying civilians—the traditional bogeyman of American political leaders since the founding of the Republic. Rather, the fear is that the thousands of changes, large and small, that the executive branch has so rapidly imposed on the military may end up undermining the professional ethic on which the U.S. form of civilian control depends.

The incoming Congress can push U.S. civil-military relations in a healthier direction by overseeing the executive branch more actively than its predecessor. It can also help restore trust with both the military and the American public if it focuses on providing greater transparency, takes a more assertive role in conducting defense policy, reinforces the military’s nonpartisan professional ethic, and establishes a new bipartisan commission to reinvigorate the military profession. Congress has the power and authority to improve civil-military relations. The new session, starting in January, is its chance to use them before the damage becomes irreversible.

When Pete Hegseth was sworn in as defense secretary, in 2025, he had strong views on the changes he wanted to make, alleging the military had lost its warfighting focus because of misguided priorities and “wokeism.” He has pursued those changes with a zeal not seen since Robert McNamara held the office from 1961 to 1968. Hegseth has reshaped the senior officer ranks: he has fired or removed nearly two dozen senior general and flag officers, and he has gotten rid of more than one-quarter of all four-star generals, the chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff, and the chiefs of the U.S. Air Force, Army, and Navy. He has fired, removed, or pulled from promotion lists approximately 80 general and flag officers, a disproportionate number of whom are women or racial minorities. Hegseth has justified those removals in the vaguest of terms, which has convinced most observers that the real reason amounts to little more than because these officers followed the lawful orders of the previous administration. No previous defense secretary has removed such large swaths of the officer corps who honored their oaths to the Constitution and faithfully implemented policies decided by their civilian superiors.

Hegseth has moved quickly, largely ignoring traditional bureaucratic practices, including rigorous staff reviews and close involvement of congressional stakeholders in changing the trajectory of the Pentagon’s policymaking and in conducting military operations. Amid ongoing questions about the legality of the boat strikes the Trump administration ordered against suspected drug traffickers last fall as well as civilian casualties in America’s war with Iran, it is critical to note that Hegseth had already fired the senior, most experienced military legal advisers—sending a warning shot across the bow of younger judge advocates—and begun a significant overhaul of the Judge Advocate General Corps. Ever since the My Lai massacre during the Vietnam War, when U.S. soldiers committed war crimes by killing hundreds of unarmed Vietnamese civilians, the JAG Corps had been empowered to help U.S. personnel abide by the laws of armed conflict. By demoting senior legal advisers and selecting less qualified personnel in those positions, however, the military’s legal voice is more marginalized today than it has been since the Vietnam War. The Trump administration has also centralized decision-making, including by placing routine actions in highly classified channels that minimize opportunities for input and deliberation.

Hegseth has done all of this—and much more—with essentially no pushback from the uniformed military or Pentagon bureaucracy. On the one hand, this shows just how strongly officers accept the principle of civilian control—a posture reinforced precisely because Hegseth has proved willing to punish even unsupported allegations of military insubordination. On the other hand, his actions have had a chilling effect across the force, creating an environment in which the military is reluctant to offer candid advice or uphold its professional standards and values when they are at odds with administration priorities. Indeed, in a highly unusual move, former government officials have publicly called for military leaders to do more to stand up to the Trump administration, even at the cost of undermining civilian control. As we argued in Foreign Affairs in 2024, however, the military cannot save democracy; only Congress and the courts can and should check presidential power.

Hegseth has enjoyed the unwavering support of President Donald Trump. But the real secret to Hegseth’s success in imposing his agenda on the Pentagon has been the unwillingness of the current Congress to exercise its Article I responsibilities or defend its traditional prerogatives. The incoming Congress can take a different approach by using its power to build trust, transparency, and accountability.

Most pressing, Congress should exercise its ability to appropriately scrutinize defense policymaking, much of which has been shrouded in secrecy under the Trump administration. The current Congress has held very few oversight hearings, and the ones that have taken place have been exchanges of insults rather than of information. When secrecy is genuinely needed, of course, Congress can issue classified briefings and hold closed hearings. But open hearings, when properly conducted, are necessary in cases in which Congress is entitled to answers, for example, about military personnel policy.

In the case of the boat strikes in the Caribbean and the eastern Pacific, Congress needs to know what advice senior military commanders and their JAGs offered, and it must understand how JAG firings affect the process by which commanders discern the lawfulness of military operations. Thus far, the Defense Department has provided only superficial explanations of its actions that basically amount to “we did this because we thought it was necessary.” Such justifications fall far short of what the Constitution envisions as the best way to hold the armed forces accountable: open debate that subjects policies to rigorous scrutiny. Greater transparency would also bolster public confidence in defense policy and remove pressure on the military to have to act as a potential savior of the Constitution.

Congress should also faithfully fulfill its “advice and consent” function established in Article II, which authorizes the Senate to review and confirm presidential nominations and promotions. Hegseth’s unprecedented intervention in the meritocratic promotion process has dramatically altered the military’s upper echelons. Congress has normalized this interference, perhaps unwittingly, by approving Hegseth’s altered promotion lists with voice votes and little inquiry.

The president and the defense secretary must retain the right to remove officers in whom they have lost confidence. But Congress has the right to know why a change was made, and it needs to ensure that promotions and removals follow established law and procedures rather than ideological or partisan considerations. Because Congress created the process for meritocratic promotions, it can push the executive branch to adhere to that process. It should mandate that the defense secretary provide a written explanation, supported by documented evidence of misconduct or derogatory information, for any off-cycle removals of senior officers and restore senior officers’ promotions, which would limit unjustified removals and uphold the merit-based selection process. Improving transparency in personnel policy will have one further salutary effect: it will help retain those qualified military personnel who are wondering whether they should give up on a military career that could be derailed by politicized and capricious promotion decisions.

In addition, Congress has a responsibility to police the well-established principle and practice that the uniformed military must stay away from partisan politics. The military has largely tried to uphold this norm, even as civilian political leaders have sought to draw it into partisan debates. Each time, for instance, Hegseth attacks the Biden administration before military audiences, most of whom served during the Biden era, he unwittingly further degrades the nonpartisan ethic on which service members’ oaths of office—and thus their subordination to his own policies—depend. It sends a message that embracing partisan rhetoric is key to career advancement and signals to the public that the military is not a neutral constituency. Congress can police the executive branch’s politicization of the military through inquiries, hearings, and even measures that prohibit the use of appropriated funds for events that treat military audiences like campaign rallies. Members of Congress can also call out for censure uniformed military personnel who behave in a blatantly partisan fashion regardless of whether they support or oppose the party the member supports.

Most ambitiously, Congress should reclaim its role in making defense policy rather than only providing oversight. Legislative deference to the executive in times of war is not unique to Trump’s second term, but Congress should close loopholes that allow presidents to expand their power unchecked. If Congress wants to provide a legal justification to allow the use of force in Iran or against drug traffickers in the Caribbean or the eastern Pacific Ocean, it should do so. If it believes these actions are unlawful, however, it must use its legislative powers to constrain them. Similarly, Congress should amend the Insurrection Act to clarify and restrict the domestic use of military force. The president must retain the ability to respond to true national emergencies, but Congress should narrow the criteria for deployment of troops on U.S. soil to avoid potential abuse.

Even as it becomes more active, Congress must strenuously avoid involving the military in a partisan war. Members of Congress should not press the military to criticize the president or the defense secretary in the made-for-social-media-outrage hearings that have become commonplace in Washington. It is right and proper for Congress to ask members of the military to share their personal and professional opinions on key strategic questions. But too often, politicians try to turn officers into combatants in their partisan battles, hiding behind the armed forces to defend partisan agendas or attacking military leaders who have simply carried out the lawful orders of a president from another party. Above all, Congress should not ask the military to be a check on civilian power. Only other civilians have the authority to do that.

Congress cannot solve longer-term issues through the day-to-day legislative process. But it has other tools to more holistically improve civil-military relations and identify the most essential reforms. The time-honored strategy is to set up a nonpartisan blue-ribbon commission to analyze problems, draw attention to their importance, and recommend solutions. A national commission on the state of the professional military ethic, co-chaired by three respected individuals—one with experience in a Republican administration, one with experience in a Democratic administration, and one retired senior military leader—would have the freedom to investigate how recent developments, including attempts to politicize the military and sustain a high tempo of operations, have affected the force. At a minimum, the commission should explore impacts on recruiting and retention; professional military education, fellowships in civilian institutions, and general and flag officer development; the JAG Corps’ ability to provide commanders with candid, independent legal advice; and the state of the National Guard.

The current Congress has not shown enough interest in exercising its own powers. The next one can make a clean break and rediscover the body’s ability to carry out the functions that are necessary to improve civil-military relations. The new Congress can demonstrate its commitment to fulfilling its responsibilities by providing ordinary legislative oversight in the raising, maintaining, and equipping of the armed forces. And on thorny issues that require a bigger response, Congress can start the reform process by creating a national commission to lay out an agenda for action. Even after two years of enormous strain that has eroded best practices in civil-military relations, the next Congress is well positioned to reverse the slide and shore up Americans’ faith in a nonpartisan, professional military that is essential to democracy.

How Far Will He Go to Make Peace—and War?

Daniel W. Drezner and Elizabeth N. Saunders

The Narrow but Feasible Path to Collective Good
